113	At 50\% (s42, earlier oracle), C1 matches KL within 0.79pp, while magnitude-trained BCE yields $2.4\times$ worse PPL (Table~\ref{tab:main}).
114	At 30\%, retrained seeds show $\leq$1.18pp agreement\footnote{s42 oracle from earlier checkpoint (gap 0.65pp); s123/s456 use seed-matched converged oracles ($\leq$0.03pp gap).}.
115	C1 demonstrates that KL-discovered mask patterns \emph{transfer} to a simpler training mechanism (PPL within 0.79pp; downstream variation up to 4.5pp MMLU); per-layer KL controls (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}) further suggest task-dependent coupling effects.
116	However, C1 also changes mask representation: binarizing oracle soft logits ($>$0) into hard masks discards ranking information, so the near-zero delta reflects transferability of binary mask \emph{patterns} rather than full predictor knowledge. BCE+Gumbel (32.22) underperforms standard BCE (27.97), indicating trajectory-dependent confounds that prevent clean isolation of target quality from gradient coupling (Appendix~\ref{app:oracle_provenance}). % exp_id: bce50_3seed, c1_bce_kl_targets_30u, c1_s123, bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, eval_bce_v3_s42

148	\end{tabular}}

150	
151	\paragraph{Relaxation, Divergence, and SPON.}
152	At 50\%, Gumbel-Sigmoid outperforms STE~\cite{bengio2013ste} by 33.8\% (11.79 vs.\ 15.77); forward KL outperforms JSD (+6.4\%) and reverse KL (+8.6\%), consistent with mode-covering~\cite{gu2024minillm}. % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42, jsd_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42
153	At 30\%, forward KL (8.81) and JSD (8.90) are near-identical, both outperforming reverse KL (9.26) and MSE (18.52$\pm$1.68). % exp_id: kl30_ablation_s42, jsd30_ablation_s42, rkl30_ablation_s42, mse30_uniform_s42, mse30_3seed
154	KL--JSD near-equivalence suggests the divergence measure matters less than optimizing through the output distribution; coupling scope may provide additional downstream benefits (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}). % exp_id: bce30_uniform_s42, rkl30_downstream_3seed
155	\paragraph{Per-Layer KL: Empirical Signal Type vs.\ Coupling Scope.}
156	\label{sec:perlayer}
157	To isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.
158	The $K$-curve (Figure~\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}) reveals three empirical patterns at 30\%: (1)~PPL is non-monotonic, with $K{=}2$ worst (10.75$\pm$0.15) due to insufficient per-layer updates and $K{=}16$ best (8.67$\pm$0.02), marginally outperforming E2E (8.81$\pm$0.02); (2)~at $K{=}1$ (no coupling), per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\pm$0.02, within 2.7\% of E2E, while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\pm$1.68 ($2.05\times$ worse); (3)~the signal type\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} gap ($K{=}1$ KL 9.05 vs.\ MSE 18.52: 9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range ($K{=}16$ 8.67 vs.\ $K{=}2$ 10.75: 2.08 PPL).
159	This suggests output-distribution alignment contributes more than coupling scope to PPL, but the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch ($K{=}1$ masks 1/32 layers during training vs.\ all 32 at inference), per-layer update count disparity, and the 50\% failure where $K{=}1$ (34.85) outperforms $K{=}4$ (44.23) while E2E reaches 11.84.
160	A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} partially deconfounds (Appendix~\ref{app:k4_downstream}): PPL and GSM8K gaps largely close while commonsense gaps (ARC-c, WG) persist, suggesting task-dependent coupling effects; detailed per-task attribution is in Appendices~\ref{app:k4_downstream}--\ref{app:perlayer_downstream}. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_50_s42, perlayer_kl_50_s42, bench_kl50_global, k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed
161	
162	\begin{figure}[t]
163	\centering
164	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{k_curve_ppl}
165	\vspace{-8pt}
166	\caption{Coupling breadth $K$ vs.\ perplexity (30\%, TEAL uniform, 3-seed mean$\pm$std). $K{=}32$ denotes end-to-end training. $K{=}2$ degradation reflects insufficient per-layer updates (${\sim}62$ vs.\ ${\sim}250$ at $K{=}8$).}
167	\label{fig:k_curve_ppl}

coupling scope may provide additional downstream benefits (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}). % exp_id: bce30_uniform_s42, rkl30_downstream_3seed\n\\paragraph{Per-Layer KL: Empirical Signal Type vs.\\ Coupling Scope.}\n\\label{sec:perlayer}\nTo isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.\nThe $K$-curve (Figure~\\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}) reveals three empirical patterns at 30\\%: (1)~PPL is non-monotonic, with $K{=}2$ worst (10.75$\\pm$0.15) due to insufficient per-layer updates and $K{=}16$ best (8.67$\\pm$0.02), marginally outperforming E2E (8.81$\\pm$0.02); (2)~at $K{=}1$ (no coupling), per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of E2E, while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse); (3)~the signal type\\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} gap ($K{=}1$ KL 9.05 vs.\\ MSE 18.52: 9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range ($K{=}16$ 8.67 vs.\\ $K{=}2$ 10.75: 2.08 PPL).\nThis suggests output-distribution alignment contributes more than coupling scope to PPL, but the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch ($K{=}1$ masks 1/32 layers during training vs.\\ all 32 at inference), per-layer update count disparity, and the 50\\% failure where $K{=}1$ (34.85) outperforms $K{=}4$ (44.23) while E2E reaches 11.84.\nA 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} partially deconfounds (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): PPL and GSM8K gaps largely close while commonsense gaps (ARC-c, WG) persist, suggesting task-dependent coupling effects; detailed per-task attribution is in Appendices~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}--\\ref{app:perlayer_downstream}. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_50_s42, perlayer_kl_50_s42, bench_kl50_global, k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{k_curve_ppl}\n\\vspace{-8pt}\n\\caption{Coupling breadth $K$ vs.\\ perplexity (30\\%, TEAL uniform, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). $K{=}32$ denotes end-to-end training. $K{=}2$ degradation reflects insufficient per-layer updates (${\\sim}62$ vs.\\ ${\\sim}250$ at $K{=}8$).}\n\\label{fig:k_curve_ppl}",
      "numLines": 20,

      "totalLines": 196
    }

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	Our findings are bounded by four limitations.
4	\textbf{Sparsity sweet spot.} 30\% is the lowest-degradation level; at 50\%, all methods collapse on reasoning tasks, and a separate predictor must be retrained per target.
5	\textbf{Knowledge-intensive regression.} KL consistently underperforms on MMLU and GSM8K across scales (8B: $-$5.2pp MMLU; 14B: $-$19.9pp GSM8K), suggesting output-distribution objectives systematically sacrifice reasoning-critical pathways.
6	\textbf{Evaluation scope.} Open-ended generation (e.g., MT-Bench) and contexts beyond 2048 tokens remain untested.
7	\textbf{Baseline coverage.} The 30\% isocompute comparison lacks a BCE 2700-step baseline; WikiText-2 evaluation shares provenance with the training corpus (WikiText-103), partially mitigated by C4 OOD results (Appendix~\ref{app:ood}).
8	
```

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "\\item \\emph{Analytical:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item \\emph{Analytical:} KL-derived masks render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).",
    "newString": "\\item \\emph{Diagnostic:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item \\emph{Practical:} KL-derived masks render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nUnlike MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm}, which applies KL to static N:M weight masks, we address contextual activation sparsity where masks must generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (mean per-layer Jaccard distance).\nA target-swap ablation shows KL-discovered mask targets carry most of the quality information: BCE retrained on KL-derived targets approaches KL PPL ($\\leq$0.65pp gap, 3-seed), while magnitude-trained BCE yields 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Per-layer controls suggest coupling effects are task-dependent, with larger impacts on commonsense than knowledge tasks (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30, both at 28.5\\%) with downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\% vs.\\ 65.3\\% dense, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with directional commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$19.9pp, $\\sigma$=5.3pp; MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study characterizing how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality across allocation strategies, sparsity levels, and model scales. The central finding---that output-distribution alignment consistently and substantially outperforms per-neuron classification for learned sparsity---is the paper's contribution. Without sparse kernel support, the predictor adds 6.4\\% latency overhead; wall-clock speedup is not addressed (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\nWhile individual components (KL divergence, Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation) are established techniques, their application to contextual activation sparsity prediction has not been explored, and the resulting empirical behaviors are non-obvious.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, with per-layer decomposition showing the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence (7--14B) with task-type split: KL outperforms on PPL and commonsense but shows systematic knowledge regression, structural to output-distribution optimization (C4 control; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments});\n\\item \\emph{Analytical:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item \\emph{Analytical:} KL-derived masks render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 22,
        "oldLines": 8,
        "newStart": 22,
        "newLines": 8,
        "lines": [
          " \\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]",
          " \\item \\emph{Empirical:} Output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, with per-layer decomposition showing the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});",
          " \\item \\emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence (7--14B) with task-type split: KL outperforms on PPL and commonsense but shows systematic knowledge regression, structural to output-distribution optimization (C4 control; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments});",
          "-\\item \\emph{Analytical:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});",
          "-\\item \\emph{Analytical:} KL-derived masks render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).",
          "+\\item \\emph{Diagnostic:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});",

    "originalFile": "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.\nWe train lightweight predictors ($<$1\\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.\nOn LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with directional commonsense gains ($n{=}3$) but knowledge-intensive regression (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp, CIs overlap; 14B GSM8K $-$19.9pp, $n{=}3$); post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.\nOutput-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns carry most of the quality information: BCE trained on KL-derived targets approaches KL-level PPL (PPL gap $\\leq$0.65pp; 3-seed, 30\\%).\nPer-layer controls show the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL) at 30\\%, though the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch; a $K{=}4$ control suggests task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.\nThis is primarily an empirical study; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here.\n",

  "new_string": "Constraint experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}) show allocation contributes: removing the learned valley improves MMLU +3.4pp and GSM8K +1.6pp. A C4-trained control (Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_control}) does not alleviate the regression (MMLU 40.0\\%, GSM8K 4.1\\% vs.\\ 40.7\\%, 7.5\\%), suggesting the deficit is structural to the optimization objective rather than training data composition.     "newString": "KL--JSD near-equivalence suggests the divergence measure matters less than optimizing through the output distribution; coupling scope may provide additional downstream benefits (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\nNotably, Gumbel relaxation harms BCE (PPL 32.22 vs.\\ 27.97), suggesting the benefit of differentiable relaxation depends on loss structure: KL's coupled Jacobian exploits Gumbel's continuous exploration, while BCE's per-neuron gradients do not.",
    "originalFile": null,

        "lines": [
          " \\paragraph{Relaxation, Divergence, and SPON.}",
          " At 50\\%, Gumbel-Sigmoid outperforms STE~\\cite{bengio2013ste} by 33.8\\% (11.79 vs.\\ 15.77); forward KL outperforms JSD (+6.4\\%) and reverse KL (+8.6\\%), consistent with mode-covering~\\cite{gu2024minillm}. % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42, jsd_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42",
          " At 30\\%, forward KL (8.81) and JSD (8.90) are near-identical, both outperforming reverse KL (9.26) and MSE (18.52$\\pm$1.68). % exp_id: kl30_ablation_s42, jsd30_ablation_s42, rkl30_ablation_s42, mse30_uniform_s42, mse30_3seed",
          "-KL--JSD near-equivalence suggests the divergence measure matters less than optimizing through the output distribution; coupling scope may provide additional downstream benefits (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}). % exp_id: bce30_uniform_s42, rkl30_downstream_3seed",
          "+KL--JSD near-equivalence suggests the divergence measure matters less than optimizing through the output distribution; coupling scope may provide additional downstream benefits (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).",
          "+Notably, Gumbel relaxation harms BCE (PPL 32.22 vs.\\ 27.97), suggesting the benefit of differentiable relaxation depends on loss structure: KL's coupled Jacobian exploits Gumbel's continuous exploration, while BCE's per-neuron gradients do not. % exp_id: bce30_uniform_s42, rkl30_downstream_3seed",
          " \\paragraph{Per-Layer KL: Empirical Signal Type vs.\\ Coupling Scope.}",
          " \\label{sec:perlayer}",
          " To isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients."

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur findings are bounded by four limitations.\n\\textbf{Sparsity sweet spot.} 30\\% is the lowest-degradation level; at 50\\%, all methods collapse on reasoning tasks, and a separate predictor must be retrained per target.\n\\textbf{Knowledge-intensive regression.} KL consistently underperforms on MMLU and GSM8K across scales (8B: $-$5.2pp MMLU; 14B: $-$19.9pp GSM8K), suggesting output-distribution objectives systematically sacrifice reasoning-critical pathways.\n\\textbf{Evaluation scope.} Open-ended generation (e.g., MT-Bench) and contexts beyond 2048 tokens remain untested.\n\\textbf{Baseline coverage.} The 30\\% isocompute comparison lacks a BCE 2700-step baseline; WikiText-2 evaluation shares provenance with the training corpus (WikiText-103), partially mitigated by C4 OOD results (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\n",

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction, outperforming BCE and MSE across five losses.
5	The KL predictor reduces PPL by 14.5\% over TEAL at 30\%, confirmed on Mistral-7B ($-$17.3\%) and Qwen-2.5-14B.
6	Gains are task-type dependent: KL improves commonsense reasoning but degrades knowledge-intensive tasks (C4 control confirms structural to KL), suggesting KL for commonsense/PPL and TEAL for knowledge tasks.
7	The signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}); disentangling these factors remains an open question.
8	Downstream differences are directional ($n{=}3$, CIs overlap). Code released upon acceptance.
9	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOutput-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction, outperforming BCE and MSE across five losses.\nThe KL predictor reduces PPL by 14.5\\% over TEAL at 30\\%, confirmed on Mistral-7B ($-$17.3\\%) and Qwen-2.5-14B.\nGains are task-type dependent: KL improves commonsense reasoning but degrades knowledge-intensive tasks (C4 control suggests structural to the optimization objective), suggesting KL for commonsense/PPL and TEAL for knowledge tasks.\nThe signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}); disentangling these factors remains an open question.\nDownstream differences are directional ($n{=}3$, CIs overlap). 两个问题需要你自己审视一下你的论文，不需要照标准答，按你对这个工作的判断回答：

**1. 你"为 rebuttal 准备的工作"，真的需要留在 rebuttal 待机区吗？**
- 哪些是你**真的预期审稿人不会问、但万一被挑战才需要拿出来**的？
- 哪些其实你自己心里就觉得"审稿人很可能想看"——那为什么不放进附录直接让人看到？

**2. 你的附录里，有没有有价值但被淹没的内容？**
- 审稿人对附录是 scan 不是 read。每个有价值的附录块，**正文里有显式 pointer 指过去吗**（"see App. X"、"Full results in Table A.N"）？
- 没有 pointer 的附录块基本等于没人看。

**唯一硬要求：** 每个想保留的附录块，正文必须有引用。没引用的，要么补引用，要么砍掉。

**汇报：** 简要说说你重新审视的结果和动了什么。不需要按统一格式，把你的判断讲清楚就行。

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01F4PcPhSbRadtqHSkBU8QFp`</summary>

```text
1	\appendix
2	
3	\section{Inference Efficiency}
4	\label{app:latency}
5	
6	Table~\ref{tab:latency} reports inference throughput on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\,GB; batch size 1, sequence length 2048, fp16, averaged over 100 forward passes).
7	
8	\begin{table}[h]
9	\centering
10	\caption{Inference latency on LLaMA-3.1-8B (RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell, batch=1, seq=2048, fp16, 100 forward passes). Overhead is relative to dense inference.}
11	\label{tab:latency}
12	\small
13	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
14	\toprule
15	Method & Sparsity & ms/fwd & tok/s & Overhead \\
16	\midrule
17	Dense & 0\% & 119.11$\pm$0.57 & 17{,}195 & --- \\
18	\midrule
19	KL predictor-only & 30\% & 126.64$\pm$0.70 & 16{,}172 & +6.3\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
20	KL predictor-only & 50\% & 126.78$\pm$0.31 & 16{,}154 & +6.4\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
21	\midrule
22	Predictor + compensation & 30\% & 129.94$\pm$0.75 & 15{,}761 & +9.1\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
23	Predictor + compensation & 50\% & 129.71$\pm$0.53 & 15{,}789 & +8.9\% \\ % exp_id: latency_bench
24	\bottomrule
25	\end{tabular}
26	\end{table}
27	
28	The full KL-sparse pipeline adds 6.4\% overhead to dense inference (126.78 vs.\ 119.11\,ms/forward), of which the predictor's 32-layer forward pass contributes 3.17\,ms (2.7\%); the remainder arises from mask application and sparse FFN indexing.
29	Eliminating the compensation network saves an additional $\sim$2.7\% latency overhead and 67.2M parameters (the per-layer GEMV required for output rescaling); output-distribution KL thus provides both quality and efficiency advantages over BCE-based approaches.
30	The overhead is near-identical at 30\% and 50\% sparsity because the predictor runs the same computation regardless of target sparsity; only the top-$k$ threshold changes.
31	
32	Note that the current implementation uses unstructured element-wise masks, which do not translate to wall-clock speedup without sparse kernel support.
33	The pipeline adds $\sim$6.4\% overhead; net acceleration requires kernel-level sparse computation optimizations (e.g., Triton block-sparse, cuSPARSE) that are beyond the scope of this work.
34	Our contribution is prediction quality and inference simplification (removing the compensation network), not end-to-end latency reduction.
35	
36	
37	\section{Predictor Bottleneck Sensitivity}
38	\label{app:db_sensitivity}
39	
40	Table~\ref{tab:db_sensitivity} reports the effect of predictor bottleneck dimension $d_b$ on PPL at 50\% sparsity under both TEAL global allocation (the paper's primary inference protocol) and per-token evaluation.
41	
42	\begin{table}[h]
43	\centering
44	\caption{Predictor bottleneck sensitivity at 50\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\downarrow$). TEAL global is the primary inference protocol; per-token shown for reference.}
45	\label{tab:db_sensitivity}
46	\small
47	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
48	\toprule
49	$d_b$ & Params & TEAL global & $\Delta$ & Per-token \\
50	\midrule
51	64 & 38.2M (0.48\%) & 12.076 & +0.9\% & 10.16 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
52	128 & 75.9M (0.95\%) & 11.963 & --- & 9.218 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
53	256 & 151.5M (1.89\%) & 12.105 & +1.2\% & 8.586 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
54	512 & 302.5M (3.77\%) & 12.315 & +2.9\% & 7.93 \\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep
55	\bottomrule
56	\end{tabular}
57	\end{table}
58	
59	Under TEAL global allocation, increasing $d_b$ beyond 128 provides no benefit (PPL slightly worsens), while per-token evaluation shows monotonic improvement (10.16$\to$7.93).
60	This divergence indicates that the global top-$k$ allocation mechanism, not predictor capacity, is the binding constraint: additional predictor capacity produces better per-neuron scores, but the global ranking and budget distribution cannot exploit them.
61	The $d_b{=}128$ configuration (75.9M params, $<$1\% of base model) is therefore optimal for the TEAL inference protocol.
62	
63	\subsection{Temperature Schedule Sensitivity}
64	\label{app:tau_sensitivity}
65	
66	\begin{table}[h]
67	\centering
68	\caption{Temperature schedule sensitivity at 50\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\downarrow$, TEAL global).}
69	\label{tab:tau_sensitivity}
70	\small
71	\begin{tabular}{lc}
72	\toprule
73	$\tau$ Schedule & PPL$\downarrow$ \\
74	\midrule
75	Fixed $\tau{=}0.5$ & \textbf{11.861} \\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep
76	Linear $1.0 \to 0.1$ (default) & 11.963 \\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep
77	Fixed $\tau{=}0.1$ & 12.072 \\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep
78	\bottomrule
79	\end{tabular}
80	\end{table}
```

109	
110	\section{Geometric Penalty Schedule Dynamics}
111	\label{app:lagrangian}
112	
113	Table~\ref{tab:lagrangian} reports the convergence of the geometric penalty schedule across sparsity targets.
114	The geometric doubling pattern ($1 \to 2 \to 4 \to 8 \to \ldots$) enables $\lambda$ to reach steady state within $\sim$10 steps; the deadzone ($\epsilon = 0.02$) then stabilizes $\lambda$ once actual sparsity is within $\pm$2\% of the target.
115	
116	\begin{table}[h]
117	\centering
118	\caption{Geometric penalty schedule convergence across sparsity targets (margin $\epsilon=0.02$, 1000 training steps). Deviation is $|$actual $-$ target$|$ / target.}
119	\label{tab:lagrangian}
120	\small
121	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
122	\toprule
123	Target $\rho$ & $\lambda_{\max}$ & Final $\lambda$ & Actual sparsity & Deviation \\
124	\midrule
125	30\% & 5000 & 5000 (cap) & 0.285 & 5.0\% \\ % exp_id: kl_30pct_s42
126	40\% & 5000 & 625 & 0.398 & 0.5\% \\ % exp_id: kl_40pct_s42
127	50\% & 1000 & 8 & 0.504 & 0.8\% \\ % exp_id: kl_50pct_s42
128	70\% & 1000 & 1000 (cap) & 0.688 & 1.7\% \\ % exp_id: kl_70pct_s42
129	\bottomrule
130	\end{tabular}
131	\end{table}
132	
133	At 50\% sparsity, the SwiGLU gate distribution already clusters near the target, so $\lambda$ remains low (8); the constraint is nearly satisfied by the initial predictor outputs.
134	At 30\% and 70\%, the gap between the gate prior and target is larger, driving $\lambda$ to its respective caps.
135	The 40\% target converges to $\lambda = 625$ with only 0.5\% deviation; this precision reflects a regime where the target is reachable without $\lambda$ saturation.
136	
137	\begin{figure}[h]
138	\centering
139	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{lagrangian_dynamics.pdf}
140	\caption{Lagrangian controller dynamics for 30\% sparsity target ($\lambda_{\max}=5000$). Left axis (log scale): penalty weight $\lambda$ rises from $0.01$ to its cap once actual sparsity undershoots the target at $\sim$step~150, exhibiting bang-bang oscillations before saturating. Right axis: actual sparsity (solid) converges from the natural density ($\approx$0.41) toward the target (dashed) and stabilizes near 0.28.}
141	\label{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}
142	\end{figure}
143	
144	Temperature $\tau$ is annealed linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over 1000 steps.
145	A mild loss increase in the final $\sim$100 steps is expected: as masks become more binary ($\tau \to 0$), discrete rounding noise slightly increases KL divergence, consistent with convergence toward the hard-mask inference regime.
146	
147	The controller reliably drives actual sparsity to within 5\% of the target across all tested regimes, with $\lambda$ spanning three orders of magnitude (8 to 5000) via geometric doubling.
148	For the 30\% target, actual sparsity is $\approx$28.5\% (5\% deviation). This does not affect conclusions: TEAL at 28.5\% actual sparsity yields PPL 10.30 (vs.\ 10.97 at exact 30\%), a 0.67 PPL difference negligible relative to the KL vs.\ TEAL gap (8.96 vs.\ 10.97).
149

170	& TEAL vanilla & 38.75 \\
171	\bottomrule

174

176	At 50\%, the KL predictor achieves 52.5\% relative advantage over TEAL (18.40 vs.\ 38.75), comparable to the 50\% advantage on WikiText-2 (11.92 vs.\ 23.90), confirming that KL-trained predictions generalize across distributions.

466	
467	\section{Taxonomy Discussion}
468	\label{app:taxonomy}
469	
470	\paragraph{Correction granularity.}
471	\emph{Static} methods~\cite{xu2025spon} learn a fixed bias vector per layer that does not depend on the input token.
472	\emph{Per-token} methods~\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward,akhauri2024shadowllm,szatkowski2024d2dmoe} produce a unique mask for each input position, conditioning on the token's hidden state at each layer.
473	
474	\paragraph{Training signal coupling.}
475	\emph{Decomposed} objectives (BCE) treat each neuron independently, optimizing per-element classification against an oracle mask.
476	\emph{Intermediate} objectives (MSE on hidden states) capture local coupling within a single layer but not end-to-end output effects.
477	\emph{Coupled} objectives (KL on output distribution) propagate gradients through the full forward pass; each mask decision's gradient reflects its joint effect with all other masks on the final distribution.
478	
479	SPON~\cite{xu2025spon} distills a per-layer bias via KL but cannot adapt to per-input neuron importance (static $\times$ coupled).
480	DejaVu~\cite{liu2023dejavu} and FastForward~\cite{gautam2026fastforward} achieve per-token granularity but use BCE, where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.
481	D2DMoE~\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} provides intermediate coupling (MSE on hidden states) but not end-to-end output effects.
482	We define \emph{output-distribution alignment} as whether the training loss optimizes a quantity that composes across layers.
483	KL on output logits directly optimizes next-token prediction loss, which does not suffer from error composition; per-layer MSE minimizes reconstruction error independently at each layer, but layer-wise optima do not compose---a mask minimizing FFN output error at layer $\ell$ may amplify errors in layers $\ell{+}1 \ldots L$.
484	The SNR analysis (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}) corroborates this: KL achieves the best masks despite $2.3\times$ lower SNR than BCE, while MSE produces the worst masks.
485	The framework predicts that losses operating on output-level quantities should outperform intermediate-level losses, a hypothesis consistent with our MSE results and testable with alternative output-level objectives.
486	
487	\section{Oracle Mask Provenance}
488	\label{app:oracle_provenance}
489	
490	C1's oracle masks are extracted from a converged KL predictor and binarized via hard threshold (logits $> 0$, equivalent to $\sigma > 0.5$); they reflect one specific training trajectory (optimizer state, data order, Gumbel noise).
491	The s42/s123/s456 consistency ($\leq$1.70pp) provides partial evidence of robustness, but does not rule out trajectory-dependent confounds.
492	
493	\section{SPON Orthogonality}
494	\label{app:spon}
495	
496	SPON~\cite{xu2025spon} bias correction is weakly correlated with the KL predictor (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038) and yields only 2.4\% PPL improvement (12.08$\to$11.79); the predictor already captures most recoverable signal. % exp_id: spon_decomposition, spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42
497	The decomposition confirms that SPON and the KL predictor operate on largely orthogonal error sources.
498	
499	\section{Per-Layer KL Downstream Decomposition}

ARC-c exhibits high seed variance ($\\sigma$=6.4pp).}\n\\label{tab:mistral_downstream}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & HS & PIQA & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense & 5.52 & 60.2 & 78.7 & 83.2 & 81.9 & 62.4 & 36.5 \\\\ % exp_id: lm_eval_mistral_dense, mistral_dense_hellaswag_piqa\nKL 30\\% & \\textbf{6.93$\\pm$0.19} & \\textbf{44.9$\\pm$6.4} & \\textbf{70.2$\\pm$1.2} & \\textbf{69.7$\\pm$0.8} & \\textbf{74.8$\\pm$0.4} & 43.3$\\pm$1.5 & 8.6$\\pm$2.2 \\\\ % exp_id: mistral_kl30_s42, mistral_kl30_s123, mistral_kl30_s456\nTEAL 30\\% & 8.38 & 41.1 & 63.5 & 60.1 & 72.1 & \\textbf{43.5} & \\textbf{15.9} \\\\ % exp_id: mistral_teal30_downstream\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Taxonomy Discussion}\n\\label{app:taxonomy}\n\n\\paragraph{Correction granularity.}\n\\emph{Static} methods~\\cite{xu2025spon} learn a fixed bias vector per layer that does not depend on the input token.\n\\emph{Per-token} methods~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward,akhauri2024shadowllm,szatkowski2024d2dmoe} produce a unique mask for each input position, conditioning on the token's hidden state at each layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Training signal coupling.}\n\\emph{Decomposed} objectives (BCE) treat each neuron independently, optimizing per-element classification against an oracle mask.\n\\emph{Intermediate} objectives (MSE on hidden states) capture local coupling within a single layer but not end-to-end output effects.\n\\emph{Coupled} objectives (KL on output distribution) propagate gradients through the full forward pass; each mask decision's gradient reflects its joint effect with all other masks on the final distribution.\n\nSPON~\\cite{xu2025spon} distills a per-layer bias via KL but cannot adapt to per-input neuron importance (static $\\times$ coupled).\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} and FastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} achieve per-token granularity but use BCE, where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nD2DMoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} provides intermediate coupling (MSE on hidden states) but not end-to-end output effects.\nWe define \\emph{output-distribution alignment} as whether the training loss optimizes a quantity that composes across layers.\nKL on output logits directly optimizes next-token prediction loss, which does not suffer from error composition; per-layer MSE minimizes reconstruction error independently at each layer, but layer-wise optima do not compose---a mask minimizing FFN output error at layer $\\ell$ may amplify errors in layers $\\ell{+}1 \\ldots L$.\nThe SNR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}) corroborates this: KL achieves the best masks despite $2.3\\times$ lower SNR than BCE, while MSE produces the worst masks.\nThe framework predicts that losses operating on output-level quantities should outperform intermediate-level losses, a hypothesis consistent with our MSE results and testable with alternative output-level objectives.\n\n\\section{Oracle Mask Provenance}\n\\label{app:oracle_provenance}\n\nC1's oracle masks are extracted from a converged KL predictor and binarized via hard threshold (logits $> 0$, equivalent to $\\sigma > 0.5$); they reflect one specific training trajectory (optimizer state, data order, Gumbel noise).\nThe s42/s123/s456 consistency ($\\leq$1.70pp) provides partial evidence of robustness, but does not rule out trajectory-dependent confounds.\n\n\\section{SPON Orthogonality}\n\\label{app:spon}\n\nSPON~\\cite{xu2025spon} bias correction is weakly correlated with the KL predictor (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038) and yields only 2.4\\% PPL improvement (12.08$\\to$11.79); the predictor already captures most recoverable signal. % exp_id: spon_decomposition, spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42\nThe decomposition confirms that SPON and the KL predictor operate on largely orthogonal error sources.\n\n\\section{Per-Layer KL Downstream Decomposition}",

204	\section{Per-Layer Retention Analysis}
205	\label{app:retention}
206	
207	\begin{figure}[h]
208	\centering
209	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{per_layer_retention_chart.pdf}
210	\caption{Per-layer neuron retention under global top-$k$ allocation at 30\%, 50\%, and 70\% sparsity. Dashed lines indicate uniform retention baselines. Late layers (L24--31) are consistently starved due to long-tailed activation distributions, with retention dropping to 28.4\% (L31) even at 30\% sparsity. At 70\%, starvation extends to mid-layers (L10--12: 16.7--20.1\%), explaining the transition to the capacity-limited regime.}
211	\label{fig:retention}
212	\end{figure}
213	
214	
215	\section{Training Stability}
216	\label{app:stability}
217	
218	Three-seed evaluation ($s \in \{42, 123, 456\}$) at 50\% sparsity under uniform allocation yields PPL $11.92 \pm 0.07$ (CV=0.6\%); training is stable across random initializations. % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform
219	Downstream metrics are similarly stable: ARC-c $33.8 \pm 1.2$\%, WinoGrande $62.9 \pm 0.1$\%, MMLU $28.0 \pm 0.6$\%, GSM8K $2.2 \pm 0.1$\%.
220	
221	Under global allocation at 50\%, MMLU is $28.3 \pm 0.7$\%; the near-random performance is capacity-limited rather than seed-sensitive ($\sigma$=0.7pp vs.\ 9.5pp at 30\% global). % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, kl50_global_s123_downstream_v2, kl50_global_s456_eval
222	The low standard deviation across both allocation strategies indicates that the KL training landscape is well-conditioned for this predictor architecture, with low sensitivity to initialization.
223	
224	\subsection{Cross-Seed Mask Consistency}
225	\label{app:cross_seed_jaccard}
226	
227	To assess whether KL predictors discover consistent mask patterns across initializations, we compute pairwise Jaccard distances among three independently trained KL predictors (s42/s123/s456) at 30\% uniform allocation (100 sequences, WikiText-2 validation). % exp_id: cross_seed_jaccard_kl30
228	
229	The mean cross-seed Jaccard distance is 0.363 (IoU = 0.637), substantially lower than the KL-vs-magnitude divergence (0.46--0.68), confirming that KL-discovered patterns form a distinct cluster from magnitude-based masks.
230	Per-layer divergence follows a U-shaped pattern: lowest at embedding-adjacent layers (L0: 0.33), peaking at early layers (L1: 0.41), with a mid-network valley (L12--14: 0.33). The random baseline Jaccard distance is 0.462.
231	
232	While specific mask configurations vary moderately across seeds (Jaccard distance 36.3\%), the resulting PPL is highly stable (8.81$\pm$0.02, CV=0.2\%), suggesting multiple near-equivalent optima in the activation mask space.
233	The cross-seed divergence (36.3\%) is substantially lower than the KL-vs-magnitude divergence (46--68\%), confirming that KL discovers effective patterns that cluster distinctly from magnitude-based selection.
234	
235	
236	\section{MSE Baseline Configuration}
237	\label{app:mse_config}
238	
239	The MSE predictor uses identical architecture (two-layer MLP, bottleneck $d_b{=}128$), training data (WikiText-103, 1000 steps), optimizer (AdamW, lr=$1{\times}10^{-3}$, weight decay $1{\times}10^{-2}$, cosine decay), Gumbel-Sigmoid mask generation ($\tau$: $1.0 \to 0.1$), and adaptive Lagrangian sparsity control as the KL and BCE predictors.
240	The only difference is the loss target: per-layer $\|\mathbf{W}_d^{(\ell)}(\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{(\ell)} \odot \mathbf{g}^{(\ell)} \odot \mathbf{x}^{(\ell)} \mathbf{W}_u^{(\ell)}) - \mathbf{y}_{\text{dense}}^{(\ell)}\|_2^2$, replacing the end-to-end KL divergence.
241	This controlled comparison isolates the effect of output-distribution alignment: all three predictors see identical data in identical order with identical architectures; only the loss function differs. % exp_id: mse30_uniform_s42, mse50_uniform_s42
242	
243	MSE uses $\lambda_{\max}{=}1000$ (vs.\ KL's 5000) because MSE's per-layer gradient magnitude is approximately $5\times$ larger than KL's end-to-end gradient, requiring proportionally less penalty pressure to achieve comparable sparsity control. Both reach their target sparsity within 110 steps.
244	At 50\% target, MSE converges to the target sparsity for seed 42; however, with seed 123, sparsity reaches only 5.1\% under the same $\lambda_{\max}$ cap (eval PPL$=$15{,}789, random-level), indicating high sensitivity to initialization---a failure mode not observed with KL-based losses.
245	At 30\%, MSE achieves 27.7\% actual sparsity for s42 (gap 2.3pp).
246	The 3-seed MSE mean (18.52$\pm$1.68; s42=20.43, s123=17.73, s456=17.39) is still $1.7\times$ worse than TEAL (10.82), confirming that the performance deficit stems from the loss function, not insufficient sparsity pressure.
247	
248	
249	\section{Extended SNR Analysis}
250	\label{app:snr_extended}
251	
252	Figure~\ref{fig:snr_trajectory} plots gradient SNR across the full 1000-step training for both KL and BCE predictors, measured at 14 checkpoints (LLaMA-3.1-8B, C4) with 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals.
253	From step 100 onward, BCE maintains consistently higher SNR than KL (2.3--17$\times$ depending on training phase), with the gap narrowing toward convergence ($2.3\times$ at step 1000: $2.026{\times}10^{-3}$ vs.\ $8.78{\times}10^{-4}$). % exp_id: snr_full_curve_1000steps
254	Despite KL's noisier gradient signal, KL-trained predictors produce substantially better masks (Table~\ref{tab:main}), confirming that \emph{target alignment} matters more than gradient clarity for mask quality.
255	BCE's higher SNR reflects its simpler per-neuron classification objective, which concentrates gradients on individual neuron decisions; KL's coupled gradient distributes signal across all mask decisions jointly, yielding lower SNR but discovering compositionally superior mask patterns (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}).
256	
257	\begin{figure}[t]
258	\centering
259	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{snr_trajectory.pdf}

620	The C4-trained predictor achieves comparable MMLU (40.0\% vs.\ 40.6\%) but worse GSM8K (4.1\% vs.\ 7.4\%) and higher PPL (11.55 vs.\ 8.82), despite training on more diverse text.
621	This rules out training data composition as the primary cause of knowledge-task regression and supports the hypothesis that forward KL's mode-covering property structurally under-protects rare reasoning-critical activations. % exp_id: c4_control_eval
622	
623	\section{Per-Layer Coupling Breadth Analysis}
624	\label{app:k_curve}
625	
626	We vary the number of layers coupled per training step ($K$) from 1 (pure per-layer) to 32 (end-to-end), evaluating all predictors under TEAL uniform allocation at 30\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B.

628	\begin{table}[h]
629	\centering
630	\caption{K-curve: coupling breadth vs.\ predictor quality (30\%, TEAL uniform, LLaMA-3.1-8B). All values are 3-seed mean$\pm$std (s42/s123/s456) except $K{=}1$ downstream (single seed). ``---'' = not evaluated.}
631	\label{tab:k_curve}
632	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
633	\begin{tabular}{cccccccc}
634	\toprule
635	$K$ & PPL$\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K & HS & PIQA \\
636	\midrule
637	1 & 9.05$\pm$0.02 & 39.9 & 65.9 & 38.6 & 5.8 & --- & --- \\
638	2 & 10.75$\pm$0.15 & 39.7$\pm$1.2 & 63.2$\pm$1.7 & 38.3$\pm$3.2 & 2.8$\pm$0.6 & 66.5$\pm$1.0 & 73.5$\pm$0.7 \\
639	4 & 8.89$\pm$0.01 & 40.0$\pm$1.2 & 66.9$\pm$0.2 & 42.7$\pm$1.4 & 7.0$\pm$1.6 & 67.8$\pm$1.0 & 74.5$\pm$0.5 \\
640	8 & 9.00$\pm$0.06 & 40.7$\pm$1.0 & 68.7$\pm$1.3 & 40.4$\pm$0.6 & 6.8$\pm$1.3 & 69.0$\pm$1.1 & 75.1$\pm$1.1 \\
641	16 & 8.67$\pm$0.02 & 41.9$\pm$1.1 & 68.5$\pm$0.9 & 40.9$\pm$2.9 & 8.5$\pm$1.2 & 70.1$\pm$0.4 & 75.0$\pm$0.8 \\

644	\end{tabular}}

647	PPL exhibits a non-monotonic U-shape (Figure~\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}): $K{=}2$ is worst (10.75$\pm$0.15), likely due to insufficient per-layer updates (${\sim}$62 updates/layer vs.\ ${\sim}$250 at $K{=}8$). $K{=}16$ achieves the lowest PPL (8.67$\pm$0.02), marginally better than E2E (8.81$\pm$0.02). $K{=}16$ MMLU exhibits high seed variance (std=2.9; s42=44.0, s123=40.0, s456=38.7), with the 3-seed mean (40.9) indistinguishable from E2E (40.7$\pm$1.3).

      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/appendix.tex",
      "content": "The C4-trained predictor achieves comparable MMLU (40.0\\% vs.\\ 40.6\\%) but worse GSM8K (4.1\\% vs.\\ 7.4\\%) and higher PPL (11.55 vs.\\ 8.82), despite training on more diverse text.\nThis rules out training data composition as the primary cause of knowledge-task regression and supports the hypothesis that forward KL's mode-covering property structurally under-protects rare reasoning-critical activations. % exp_id: c4_control_eval\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Coupling Breadth Analysis}\n\\label{app:k_curve}\n\nWe vary the number of layers coupled per training step ($K$) from 1 (pure per-layer) to 32 (end-to-end), evaluating all predictors under TEAL uniform allocation at 30\\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{K-curve: coupling breadth vs.\\ predictor quality (30\\%, TEAL uniform, LLaMA-3.1-8B). All values are 3-seed mean$\\pm$std (s42/s123/s456) except $K{=}1$ downstream (single seed). ``---'' = not evaluated.}\n\\label{tab:k_curve}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{cccccccc}\n\\toprule\n$K$ & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K & HS & PIQA \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & 9.05$\\pm$0.02 & 39.9 & 65.9 & 38.6 & 5.8 & --- & --- \\\\\n2 & 10.75$\\pm$0.15 & 39.7$\\pm$1.2 & 63.2$\\pm$1.7 & 38.3$\\pm$3.2 & 2.8$\\pm$0.6 & 66.5$\\pm$1.0 & 73.5$\\pm$0.7 \\\\\n4 & 8.89$\\pm$0.01 & 40.0$\\pm$1.2 & 66.9$\\pm$0.2 & 42.7$\\pm$1.4 & 7.0$\\pm$1.6 & 67.8$\\pm$1.0 & 74.5$\\pm$0.5 \\\\\n8 & 9.00$\\pm$0.06 & 40.7$\\pm$1.0 & 68.7$\\pm$1.3 & 40.4$\\pm$0.6 & 6.8$\\pm$1.3 & 69.0$\\pm$1.1 & 75.1$\\pm$1.1 \\\\\n16 & 8.67$\\pm$0.02 & 41.9$\\pm$1.1 & 68.5$\\pm$0.9 & 40.9$\\pm$2.9 & 8.5$\\pm$1.2 & 70.1$\\pm$0.4 & 75.0$\\pm$0.8 \\\\\n32 (E2E) & 8.81$\\pm$0.02 & 43.7$\\pm$2.5 & 68.9$\\pm$0.4 & 40.7$\\pm$1.3 & 7.5$\\pm$0.1 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nPPL exhibits a non-monotonic U-shape (Figure~\\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}): $K{=}2$ is worst (10.75$\\pm$0.15), likely due to insufficient per-layer updates (${\\sim}$62 updates/layer vs.\\ ${\\sim}$250 at $K{=}8$). $K{=}16$ achieves the lowest PPL (8.67$\\pm$0.02), marginally better than E2E (8.81$\\pm$0.02). $K{=}16$ MMLU exhibits high seed variance (std=2.9; s42=44.0, s123=40.0, s456=38.7), with the 3-seed mean (40.9) indistinguishable from E2E (40.7$\\pm$1.3).\nThe signal type gap ($K{=}1$ KL 9.05 vs.\\ per-layer MSE 18.52$\\pm$1.68) of 9.47 PPL exceeds the coupling breadth range within KL (best $K{=}16$ 8.67 vs.\\ worst $K{=}2$ 10.75 = 2.08 PPL), reinforcing that output-distribution alignment, not coupling scope, is the primary quality lever for PPL at this sparsity level. % exp_id: k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed, perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed\n\n\\section{Licenses}\n\\label{app:licenses}\n\nLLaMA-3.1-8B is released under the Llama 3.1 Community License Agreement.\nMistral-7B-v0.3 is released under the Apache 2.0 License.\nQwen-2.5-14B is released under the Apache 2.0 License.\nWikiText-103 and WikiText-2~\\cite{merity2017pointer} are released under the Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike License (CC BY-SA 3.0).\nAll evaluation benchmarks (ARC, WinoGrande, MMLU, GSM8K, HellaSwag, PIQA) are publicly available under their respective licenses.\nOur code will be released under the MIT License upon acceptance.\n",

570	\end{tabular}
571	\end{table}
572	
573	Protecting late layers (L22--L28) does not improve knowledge-intensive metrics despite reducing their sparsity from 48--57\% to $\leq$30\%, while worsening PPL by 5.2\%.
574	Conversely, removing the protection valley (forcing L14--L18 to 30\% instead of 14--18\%) improves MMLU by +3.4pp and GSM8K by +1.6pp at the cost of ARC-c $-$1.5pp.
575	The KL predictor's learned allocation thus represents a task-dependent trade-off---prioritizing commonsense reasoning via middle-layer protection---rather than a uniformly optimal pattern.
576	This allocation trade-off also explains the $K{=}4$ MMLU anomaly (\S\ref{app:k4_downstream}): $K{=}4$'s near-uniform learned sparsity (std=0.003) inherently removes the valley, producing MMLU=43.5 comparable to the remove-valley condition (44.0).
577	
578	\section{Per-Layer Sparsity Distribution}
579	\label{app:perlayer_sparsity}
580	
581	The KL predictor at 30\% target sparsity learns a non-uniform per-layer allocation that differs markedly from magnitude-based selection.
582	Aggregate sparsity is similar across broad regions (early L0--L7: KL 28.1\% vs.\ magnitude 27.5\%; late L24--L31: 44.9\% vs.\ 47.2\%), but middle layers (L8--L23) exhibit high internal variance despite similar averages (28.2\% vs.\ 27.3\%).
583	Table~\ref{tab:perlayer_sparsity} highlights three sub-ranges.
584	
585	\begin{table}[h]
586	\centering
587	\caption{Per-layer sparsity sub-ranges: KL predictor vs.\ magnitude (LLaMA-3.1-8B, 30\%, uniform allocation). Ranges denote min--max across layers in each group.}
588	\label{tab:perlayer_sparsity}
589	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
590	\toprule
591	Layer Range & KL (\%) & Mag (\%) & $\Delta$ (pp) \\
592	\midrule
593	L8--L12 (high pruning) & 32--36 & 25--26 & $+$7 to $+$10 \\
594	L14--L18 (protection valley) & 14--18 & 28--30 & $-$8 to $-$16 \\
595	L22--L28 (aggressive pruning) & 48--57 & --- & --- \\

598	\end{table}

600	\section{C4 Training Data Control}
601	\label{app:c4_control}

### Tool result

_source line 864 · 2026-05-24T05:03:39.942Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01VeCeC1mpSGRN7cr7nnzgD1`</summary>

```text
28	At 30\% sparsity, output-distribution KL under uniform allocation achieves PPL 8.81$\pm$0.02 (3-seed), a 14.5\% reduction over TEAL at matched actual sparsity (8.81 vs.\ 10.30, both at 28.5\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ood}).
29	Uniform allocation yields stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\pm$1.3\%, 3-seed; Appendix~\ref{app:stability}), while global top-$k$ exhibits high seed variance (35.7$\pm$9.5\%) from late-layer starvation (L31 retention 28.4\%; \S\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_s123_uniform, kl30_s456_uniform, kl30_3seed
30	
31	\paragraph{Task-Type Split.}
32	KL shows directional improvements over TEAL on commonsense reasoning (ARC-c, WG, HellaSwag, PIQA: 4/4 wins at 30\% uniform) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive tasks (MMLU, GSM8K: 0/2).
33	With only three seeds, 95\% confidence intervals for individual downstream tasks overlap; we therefore treat PPL ($\sigma{=}0.02$, non-overlapping) as the primary metric and downstream differences as directional evidence.
34	The KL predictor protects L14--L18 (14--18\% sparsity vs.\ 32\% global mean) while aggressively pruning L22--L28 (48--57\%). Constraint interventions (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}) confirm a task-dependent trade-off: removing the valley improves MMLU +3.4pp but reduces ARC-c by 1.5pp, consistent with localized factual associations~\cite{geva2023dissecting}; task-aware allocation may resolve this. % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, teal30_uniform, hellaswag_piqa_kl30_uniform, perlayer_sparsity_analysis, constraint_protect_late, constraint_remove_valley
35	
36	\paragraph{Knowledge-Task Regression: Hypothesis.}
37	Constraint experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}) show allocation contributes: removing the learned valley improves MMLU +3.4pp and GSM8K +1.6pp. A C4-trained control (Appendix~\ref{app:c4_control}) does not alleviate the regression (MMLU 40.0\%, GSM8K 4.1\% vs.\ 40.7\%, 7.5\%), suggesting the deficit is structural to the optimization objective rather than training data composition. Both loss-inherent (mode-covering sacrifices rare activations) and allocation-induced factors likely contribute; disentangling them requires neuron-level attribution beyond our scope. % exp_id: constraint_remove_valley, c4_control_eval
```

</details>

194	Consistent regression across scales (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp, 14B GSM8K 32.2$\pm$5.3 vs.\ 52.1) suggests systematic limitations of output-distribution KL.         "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " To isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.",
          " The $K$-curve (Figure~\\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}) reveals three empirical patterns at 30\\%: (1)~PPL is non-monotonic, with $K{=}2$ worst (10.75$\\pm$0.15) due to insufficient per-layer updates and $K{=}16$ best (8.67$\\pm$0.02), marginally outperforming E2E (8.81$\\pm$0.02); (2)~at $K{=}1$ (no coupling), per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of E2E, while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse); (3)~the signal type\\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} gap ($K{=}1$ KL 9.05 vs.\\ MSE 18.52: 9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range ($K{=}16$ 8.67 vs.\\ $K{=}2$ 10.75: 2.08 PPL).",
          " This suggests output-distribution alignment contributes more than coupling scope to PPL, but the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch ($K{=}1$ masks 1/32 layers during training vs.\\ all 32 at inference), per-layer update count disparity, and the 50\\% failure where $K{=}1$ (34.85) outperforms $K{=}4$ (44.23) while E2E reaches 11.84.",
          "-A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} partially deconfounds (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): PPL and GSM8K gaps largely close while commonsense gaps (ARC-c, WG) persist, suggesting task-dependent coupling effects; detailed per-task attribution is in Appendices~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}--\\ref{app:perlayer_downstream}. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_50_s42, perlayer_kl_50_s42, bench_kl50_global, k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed",
          "+A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} partially deconfounds (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): PPL and GSM8K gaps largely close while commonsense gaps (ARC-c, WG) persist, suggesting task-dependent coupling effects; detailed per-task attribution is in Appendices~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}--\\ref{app:perlayer_downstream}; full K-curve downstream in Appendix~\\ref{app:k_curve}. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_50_s42, perlayer_kl_50_s42, bench_kl50_global, k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed",

109	This coupling allows KL to evaluate each neuron's importance in the context of the full sparse computation, rather than against a fixed magnitude target.
110	
111	Gradient SNR measurements\footnote{$\text{SNR}_\ell = \|\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\theta_\ell} \mathcal{L}]\|_2^2 / \text{Var}[\nabla_{\theta_\ell} \mathcal{L}]$, expectation over mini-batches.} reveal that BCE maintains consistently higher SNR than KL throughout training ($2.3\times$ at convergence; Figure~\ref{fig:snr_trajectory}), indicating that KL's quality advantage does not arise from cleaner gradient signals.
112	Rather, KL's end-to-end coupled gradient serves as a \emph{search mechanism} that discovers compositionally superior mask patterns, even when the per-step signal is noisier than BCE's decomposed updates.
113	The target-swap ablation in \S\ref{sec:mask_analysis} suggests that the discovered mask targets, not the coupling mechanism itself, drive inference-stage quality.
```

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Output-distribution KL divergence vs BCE for contextual sparsity prediction in SwiGLU LLMs

   - 3-seed evaluation (s42, s123, s456) with mean±std reporting
   - Three structural criticals spanning 6-11 review rounds: §4.5 train-inference mismatch confound, knowledge regression mechanism (mode-covering vs allocation-induced), downstream statistical reliability (n=3)

   - Statistical significance of downstream gaps: t≈6.9 (p<0.01) for MMLU, t≈6.5 for GSM8K — gaps ARE significant despite small n
   - "CIs overlap" was potentially misleading for uniform allocation where CIs don't actually overlap with TEAL

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **paper/conclusion.tex** — Critical for page compliance. Condensed from overflow to fit page 8.
     ```latex
     \section{Conclusion}
     \label{sec:conclusion}
     
     Output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction, outperforming BCE and MSE across five losses.
     The KL predictor reduces PPL by 14.5\% over TEAL at 30\%, confirmed on Mistral-7B ($-$17.3\%) and Qwen-2.5-14B.
     Gains are task-type dependent: KL improves commonsense reasoning but degrades knowledge-intensive tasks (C4 control suggests structural to the optimization objective), suggesting KL for commonsense/PPL and TEAL for knowledge tasks.
     The signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
     Downstream differences are directional ($n{=}3$); code released upon acceptance.
     ```

   - **paper/introduction.tex** — Changed contribution labels
     ```latex
     \item \emph{Diagnostic:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\S\ref{sec:perlayer});
     \item \emph{Practical:} KL-derived masks render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\S\ref{sec:compensation}).

      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOutput-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction, outperforming BCE and MSE across five losses.\nThe KL predictor reduces PPL by 14.5\\% over TEAL at 30\\%, confirmed on Mistral-7B ($-$17.3\\%) and Qwen-2.5-14B.\nGains are task-type dependent: KL improves commonsense reasoning but degrades knowledge-intensive tasks (C4 control suggests structural to the optimization objective), suggesting KL for commonsense/PPL and TEAL for knowledge tasks.\nThe signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\nDownstream differences are directional ($n{=}3$); code released upon acceptance.\n",
        "numLines": 9,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 9
      }
    },

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/limitations.tex",
        "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur findings are bounded by four limitations.\n\\textbf{Sparsity sweet spot.} 30\\% is the lowest-degradation level; at 50\\%, all methods collapse on reasoning tasks, and a separate predictor must be retrained per target.\n\\textbf{Knowledge-intensive regression.} KL consistently underperforms on MMLU and GSM8K across scales (8B: $-$5.2pp MMLU; 14B: $-$19.9pp GSM8K), suggesting output-distribution objectives systematically sacrifice reasoning-critical pathways.\n\\textbf{Evaluation scope.} Open-ended generation (e.g., MT-Bench) and contexts beyond 2048 tokens remain untested.\n\\textbf{Baseline coverage.} The 30\\% isocompute comparison lacks a BCE 2700-step baseline; WikiText-2 evaluation shares provenance with the training corpus (WikiText-103), partially mitigated by C4 OOD results (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}). MSE uses identical hyperparameters to KL; its gradient magnitude is ${\\sim}5\\times$ larger, and performance may improve with a dedicated learning rate.\n",
        "numLines": 8,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 8
      }
    },

{
  "parentUuid": "0db2a1a6-a6bc-4e60-934a-be2d59a7f6b0",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/abstract.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.\nWe train lightweight predictors ($<$1\\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.\nOn LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with directional commonsense gains ($n{=}3$) but knowledge-intensive regression (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp; 14B GSM8K $-$19.9pp; directional at $n{=}3$); post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.\nOutput-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns carry most of the quality information: BCE trained on KL-derived targets approaches KL-level PPL (PPL gap $\\leq$0.65pp; 3-seed, 30\\%).\nPer-layer controls show the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL) at 30\\%, though the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch; a $K{=}4$ control suggests task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.\nThis is primarily an empirical study; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here.\n",
        "numLines": 8,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 8
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/abstract.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",
  "uuid": "99d20777-f3ea-4091-86b0-27afa66140c9",

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "99d20777-f3ea-4091-86b0-27afa66140c9",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nUnlike MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm}, which applies KL to static N:M weight masks, we address contextual activation sparsity where masks must generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (mean per-layer Jaccard distance).\nA target-swap ablation shows KL-discovered mask targets carry most of the quality information: BCE retrained on KL-derived targets approaches KL PPL ($\\leq$0.65pp gap, 3-seed), while magnitude-trained BCE yields 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Per-layer controls suggest coupling effects are task-dependent, with larger impacts on commonsense than knowledge tasks (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30, both at 28.5\\%) with downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\% vs.\\ 65.3\\% dense, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with directional commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$19.9pp, $\\sigma$=5.3pp; MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study characterizing how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality across allocation strategies, sparsity levels, and model scales. The central finding---that output-distribution alignment consistently and substantially outperforms per-neuron classification for learned sparsity---is the paper's contribution. Without sparse kernel support, the predictor adds 6.4\\% latency overhead; wall-clock speedup is not addressed (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\nWhile individual components (KL divergence, Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation) are established techniques, their application to contextual activation sparsity prediction has not been explored, and the resulting empirical behaviors are non-obvious.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, with per-layer decomposition showing the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence (7--14B) with task-type split: KL outperforms on PPL and commonsense but shows systematic knowledge regression, structural to output-distribution optimization (C4 control; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments});\n\\item \\emph{Diagnostic:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item \\emph{Practical:} KL-derived masks render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",
        "numLines": 35,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 35
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/introduction.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",

### Tool result

_source line 909 · 2026-05-24T05:07:09.004Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Prq9hd97azPBSemeDwYmH5`</summary>

```text
105	
106	Under BCE, the predictor trains against oracle masks $m_j^* = \mathbf{1}[|g_j| \geq \text{top-}k\text{ threshold}]$ derived from activation magnitudes.
107	The gradient $\partial \mathcal{L}_{\text{BCE}} / \partial s_j = \sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$; each neuron is trained independently with no inter-neuron coupling.
108	Under output-distribution KL, the chain rule introduces a downstream Jacobian $\partial \mathbf{z}_{\text{sparse}} / \partial \tilde{m}_j^{(\ell)}$ that couples neuron $j$'s gradient to all other retained neurons in layers $\ell$ through $L$, because the sparse hidden state propagates through subsequent attention and FFN operations.
109	This coupling allows KL to evaluate each neuron's importance in the context of the full sparse computation, rather than against a fixed magnitude target.
110	
111	Gradient SNR measurements\footnote{$\text{SNR}_\ell = \|\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\theta_\ell} \mathcal{L}]\|_2^2 / \text{Var}[\nabla_{\theta_\ell} \mathcal{L}]$, expectation over mini-batches.} reveal that BCE maintains consistently higher SNR than KL throughout training ($2.3\times$ at convergence; Figure~\ref{fig:snr_trajectory}), indicating that KL's quality advantage does not arise from cleaner gradient signals.
112	Rather, KL's end-to-end coupled gradient serves as a \emph{search mechanism} that discovers compositionally superior mask patterns, even when the per-step signal is noisier than BCE's decomposed updates.
113	The target-swap ablation in \S\ref{sec:mask_analysis} suggests that the discovered mask targets, not the coupling mechanism itself, drive inference-stage quality.
114	
115	\subsection{Two-Dimensional Taxonomy of Sparsity Prediction Methods}
116	\label{sec:method:taxonomy}
117	
118	We organize prior work along two axes: \emph{correction granularity} (how the mask adapts to input) and \emph{training signal coupling} (whether the objective captures inter-neuron dependencies).
119	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01X5Bey3KCjEpxU7JhW3pJ5q`</summary>

```text
111:Gradient SNR measurements\footnote{$\text{SNR}_\ell = \|\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\theta_\ell} \mathcal{L}]\|_2^2 / \text{Var}[\nabla_{\theta_\ell} \mathcal{L}]$, expectation over mini-batches.} reveal that BCE maintains consistently higher SNR than KL throughout training ($2.3\times$ at convergence; Figure~\ref{fig:snr_trajectory}; extended analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:snr_extended}), indicating that KL's quality advantage does not arise from cleaner gradient signals.
```

```text
29:Uniform allocation yields stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\pm$1.3\%, 3-seed; Appendix~\ref{app:stability}), while global top-$k$ exhibits high seed variance (35.7$\pm$9.5\%) from late-layer starvation (L31 retention 28.4\%, Appendix~\ref{app:retention}; \S\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_s123_uniform, kl30_s456_uniform, kl30_3seed
32:KL shows directional improvements over TEAL on commonsense reasoning (ARC-c, WG, HellaSwag, PIQA: 4/4 wins at 30\% uniform; Appendix~\ref{app:downstream}) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive tasks (MMLU, GSM8K: 0/2).
34:[Omitted long matching line]
160:[Omitted long matching line]
173:FastForward-style compensation~\cite{gautam2026fastforward} (+67.2M params) provides no benefit (Appendices~\ref{app:comp_convergence}--\ref{app:training_convergence}); SPON bias correction is orthogonal (Appendix~\ref{app:spon}). Predictor capacity and temperature sensitivity in Appendix~\ref{app:db_sensitivity}. % exp_id: kl_comp_30_s42, kl_comp_30_s456, kl_comp_50_s42, bce_comp_staged_s42, db_sensitivity_sweep, tau_schedule_sweep, training_steps_sweep
193:On Mistral-7B-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral}, PPL generalizes ($-$17.3\%, 3-seed; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_arch}); on Qwen-2.5-14B~\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, PPL generalizes but MMLU/GSM8K decline (Appendix~\ref{app:14b}).
```

Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps.
